The people will lie abourd the shipps 2 or 3 daies &amp; traffique w<sup>th</sup> you, but are very subtill in their trade, they must be vsed very kindlie &amp; they will tell you all thinge how to bewraye the Portugalls.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">A present.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">A present is needfull to be sent to the k. of Samarins, being a man of verie greate power &amp; noe freind to the Portugall but an enimie, and heere many thinge will be brought you from the shoare of all kinde of stones &amp; pearles, &amp; other good thinge. A present for this k. of the Samarins would be verie needfull w<sup>ch</sup> should be of theis thinge ffollowinge. Guilded head peece, some Muskette, &amp; 5 or 6 barrels of powder.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Theis English wares ffollowinge are good to sell theare.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">English wares.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Lead, Tynn, Iron, peece of 8, pouder, rapiers, &amp; head peeces</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The kinge [of] Canano<sup>r</sup> is greater than the kinge of Callicutte and is thought to be noe freinde w<sup>th</sup> the Portugalls but rather an enimie &amp; hath alsoe warrs w<sup>th</sup> the kinge of Callicutte.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">the k. of Callicutt.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">The Cape Commorim.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Selland.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The k. of Callicutt is in subiection to the Portugalls therefore beware <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-176" data-page="176" data-folio="84" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n175_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 84">[p. 84]</button> ffrom <em>Callicut</em> saile towards the <em>Cape of Comorem</em> but edg out of the sight of the land to the Sea that you might not come in sight of the Cape of <em>Comorĕm</em> &amp; soe take towardes <em>Selland</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-146" href="#sec-04-fn-146" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-146" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> not comeing too neere into the bay because of an indraught that sett in: and take yo<sup>r</sup> Course straight to the riuer of <em>Columba</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-147" href="#sec-04-fn-147" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-147" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> in <em>Selland</em> where theare standę a small forte of noe force, neither can yt annoy you.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Heare you shall buie theis Cōmodities followinge of the Inhabitantę of <em>Selland</em> not of the Portugalls in noe wyse.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Indie wares</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">English wares</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Synamon</em> for this is the onelie place for <em>Synaman</em>, pearles Rubies &amp; some other stones. ffor theis stones you may sell theare theis Cōmodities ffollowinge, ffine Callicoes, powder &amp; peecę lead &amp; Tynn.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">How to choose Synamon.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Take yo<sup>r</sup> <em>Synamon</em> and beate yt in a morter to fine powder when yo<sup>u</sup> haue soe done, sett a pott of water vpon the fier &amp; lett yt seeth then put in a handfull or twoe of yo<sup>r</sup> beaten Synamon &amp; lett yt seeth a while, &amp; yf yt hange together like lyme it is naught but yf yt be good it will not hang together.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">From Selland to Sumatra and to Bantam</aside></div><p class="prose-p">ffrom the Coast of Selland saile to <em>Sumatra</em>, &amp; soe come in sight of the land, sayling all alonge the Coste keepinge the land in sight, vntill you come neere the sholes, w<sup>ch</sup> sholes lie in the height of 2 degrees to the Southward of the equinoctiall. Then edge of to the Seaward for feare of the sholes then being cleere of them put into the mainland againe goinge w<sup>thin</sup> the Iland &amp; soe alonge all the Coastę, till you come to the straightę of Sonday &amp; soe to Bantam.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Comodities of Cowcheen neere Callicutt.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Guzaratts</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Att Cowcheen<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-148" href="#sec-04-fn-148" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-148" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> w<sup>ch</sup> is a place neere Callicutt is stoare of pepp diu<sup>r</sup>s kindę of Goomes Pearles Diamondę Rubies &amp; Bezar stones All w<sup>ch</sup> are brought from Goa &amp; more quantitie then att Goa M<sup>r</sup> C. saith for truth that all the Countrie of Guzarattę &amp; the k. himselfe, are greatelie subiect to the Portugalls and dare not doe any thinge to displease <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-177" data-page="177" data-folio="85" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n176_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 85">[p. 85]</button> the Portugalls. And alsoe the Guzaratte do saile in the Portugalls shipps in eu<sup>r</sup>y pte of the East Indies therefore he thinketh that the Guzaratte &amp; Portugalls will rather betray vs then deale honestlie w<sup>th</sup> vs.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Bengalla</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Mesopotania Cheramandalla St Thome.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In all the Countrie of Bengalla (wherein <em>Mosopotania</em>,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-149" href="#sec-04-fn-149" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-149" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> is a Cheefe Cittie) are made all the stuffes that we Carrie to the <em>Moloccos</em> &amp; are there to be had exceeding Cheepe, theis blew peeces of 14 yards as m<sup>r</sup> C. saith are to be bought in Bengalla for ¾ a Royall of 8 or 3 of them for 2 Royalls of 8, but theis stuffe are made cheeflye vp in the Countrie frome the Townes <em>St Thome</em> &amp; <em>Cheramandalla</em> are alsoe in Bengalla, &amp; there are all those Pinthados &amp; other stuffe made w<sup>ch</sup> we carrie to <em>Moloccos</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">English wares as gaddes of Steele Tynn.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Free trade in Mesopotania.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In Bengalla we vtter theis englishe Comodities Ryalls of 8 cheeflie, Iron small gadde<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-150" href="#sec-04-fn-150" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-150" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> of steele Tynn in small barrs, or little peeces &amp; leade he knoweth not whether English Cloth will sell, all kinde of munition will sell exceedinge well in Bengalla &amp; mesopotania Theis people are not subiect to the Portugalls, but the Portugalls dwell in <em>Mesopotamia</em> &amp; soe haue the ffleminge a factorie theare, and all men may traffique here freelie.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">att Betania English cloth, kersies.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">There are many kingdomes in Bengalla, but the Cittie of <em>Mesopotamia</em> is the Cheeffest Cittie <em>English</em> cloth or rather kersies doe sell viz: Stamells venice redde, &amp; blewes.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Ternata of the rest of the Ilandes and trade of the Moloccos read at larg in my other booke written onlie of the Molloccos.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Head peeces that are full of Studde &amp; a place to put a fether behinde and alsoe some grauen &amp; some guilt will sell in barter for Cloues exceeding well: alsoe longe peeces dagge<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-151" href="#sec-04-fn-151" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-151" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> plate of Copper such as we bring from Hambrowe<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-152" href="#sec-04-fn-152" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-152" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> yf you sould not aboue 4 or 500 peeces will sell well, but you <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-178" data-page="178" data-folio="86" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n177_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 86">[p. 86]</button> must lett them see but a few att a tyme, heere the people must be kindlie vsed and you shall haue freindshipp. But o<sup>r</sup> ffactor Thomas Brown [e] being Churlish &amp; rude to them, they would not deale w<sup>th</sup> him but foboure to our greate losse for he thought he should be churlish to them as he was att Bantam, but they people are not such.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">How to preserue Iron tooles from rustinge.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Take apinte of Sallet oyle &amp; a pound of Leade dust, being verie fine you must haue the finest file as you can gett to make the dust small, mingle the said dust and Oyle together apound of leade w<sup>th</sup> a pinte Oyle, being thus mingled lett them stand amonth and the leade will be dissolued into the Oyle Then take a Cloth &amp; dipp into the Oyle &amp; wipe the edg tooles therew<sup>th</sup> &amp; yt will keepe them or any Iron, &amp; steele from rustinge.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">A passe to Iohn Knight for discouery of the Norwest passadge.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">TO ALL THOSE<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-153" href="#sec-04-fn-153" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-153" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> to whome theis presente shall come of what degree &amp; Condic̃on soeu<sup>r</sup> we the Companie of English marchaunte for the discou<sup>r</sup>y of new trades, &amp; of the East India Companie send greetinge Whereas w<sup>th</sup> the Consent &amp; likeing of his ma<sup>tie</sup> &amp; the priuie councell, we haue intertayned the bearer hereof Iohn Knight &amp; his Companie to vndertake and attempte the pformaunce &amp; discou<sup>r</sup>y of c<sup>r</sup>teyne places, as we haue geuen him Order, &amp; haue prepared and solie Comitted a small shipp called the hopewell vnto the Cardge of the said Iohn Knight to the same end &amp; purpose &amp; to noe other effect. Theis are therefore to intreate &amp; desier you, &amp; eu<sup>r</sup>y of you to pmitt &amp; suffer the said Iohn Knight w<sup>th</sup> the said shipp &amp; Companie freelie and quietlie to passe w<sup>th</sup>out any yo<sup>r</sup> lett <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-179" data-page="179" data-folio="87" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n178_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 87">[p. 87]</button> hindraunce or molestac̃on. In testimonie whereof wee haue caused the seu<sup>r</sup>all seales of o<sup>r</sup> Companies herevnto to be fixed. London the 10<sup>th</sup> of Aprill 1606. And in the 4<sup>th</sup> yeare of the reigne of o<sup>r</sup> Sou<sup>r</sup>eigne Lo: Iames by the grace of God k. of greate Brittan ffraunce &amp; Ireland defendo<sup>r</sup> of the faith &amp;c:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Hearas</span> we [are] aduised that one of o<sup>r</sup> East India shipps<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-154" href="#sec-04-fn-154" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-154" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> is entred into the Porte of Plymoth, one other neare the Ile of wight: &amp; the 3 lefte in the soundingē &amp; we doe not knowe what state the said shipps are in but doubt they are weeke of men victuals and other p̄visions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">A Comission to goe to the ships and prouide necessaries &amp;c.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">and 2 others to other portes</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Theis men had also warrants wth them from ·m<sup>r</sup> Garway to the farmers of the Coaste for prouision of money.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">ffor better Comforte and releueing of them and their wantē we haue intreated you <strong>A B</strong> &amp; <strong>C D</strong> w<sup>th</sup> all expedic̃on to repaier to the said porte of Plimoth or there aboute where any of the said shipps shall be arriued, &amp; doe your best endevo<sup>rs</sup> aswell to puide for such men as you shall finde to be sicke &amp; not not able to endure the present passadge by Sea: as alsoe all thingē w<sup>ch</sup> shall be necessarie for the rest of the men and for p̄visions of all wantē of of the said shipps. And for yo<sup>r</sup> better Assistaunce herein (yf need be) you are in the Companies name to intreate any lawfull favo<sup>r</sup> from the Officers of his ma<sup>ties</sup> shipps, &amp; others for men Anchors Cables &amp;c: And furder that nothinge be ymbecilled taken carried or conveyed out of the said shipps by any p̄son whatsoeu<sup>r</sup>: wee pray you to make yo<sup>r</sup> retourne backe againe in such shipp as you shall soe first light on for the more ease of such as came in the same now wearied w<sup>th</sup> longe &amp; tedious trouble of Sea &amp; weather. Prouided yf you shall happen to light of 2 of the said shipps in one place or not farr distaunt. That then in such case we desire you to deuide yo<sup>r</sup> selues each to either shipp for prouiding &amp; doing as aforesaid. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-180" data-page="180" data-folio="88" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n179_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 88">[p. 88]</button> And alsoe yf you shall heeare of any thinge to be taken out of the said shipps by any whosoeu<sup>r</sup>, you shall vse the best meanes you may for recou<sup>r</sup>y &amp; obtayneing thereof agayne for thuse of the Companie. And whatsoeu<sup>r</sup> you shall doe in effectinge of this busines for the good of the Companie we doe by this o<sup>r</sup> Comission ratifie Confirme &amp; allow. Giuen vnder the seale of o<sup>r</sup> Companie the 7<sup>th</sup> of May 1606.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">The agreemt wth the porters.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Wee the</span> Comitties for the East India Companie: &amp; the principalls of the Porters whose names are heerevnder subscribed doe Couenaunte and agree in forme followinge. Viz: That the said Comitties doe pmyse, in the name of the said Companye to pay vnto the said porters for slinging, Craying, loading in &amp; out of Carte, carrying, wayghing, &amp; stoweing in the warehowse or Sello<sup>rs</sup> appointed for the said Companies goode, and all other thinge necessary to this effect the some of for eu<sup>r</sup>y Hoggeshed, &amp; for eu<sup>r</sup>y bagg baile &amp; Canister the some of . . In Considerac̃on of w<sup>ch</sup> seu<sup>r</sup>all somes the said Porters for them &amp; eu<sup>r</sup>y of them Covenant pmyse &amp; agree to and w<sup>th</sup> the said Comitties. That they the said Porters shall att their own pp chardge slinge Craine lade in &amp; and out of Carte &amp; Carte, &amp; Carrie weighe and stowe the said Companies goode now to be vnladen out of the shipps latelie arriued, or cause the same to be done in manner as aforesaid And alsoe to satisfie the said Companie for whatsoeu<sup>r</sup> damadge shall happen to the same goode by the default or ymbecilm<sup>t</sup> of the said Porters or any other whome they shall appointe to this busines, either in Craying or while the same goode shall be vnder their power or service to be done in the premisses. And furder to stow &amp; lade on bourde the lighters all such emptie Caske, and other thinge as shall be needfull to be ymployed for puision for the same goode, w<sup>th</sup>out demaundeinge any thinge for the same. And moreou<sup>r</sup> for the better satisfacc̃on &amp; indemnypnitie of the said Companie shall before they doe ymploye any pson whatsoeu<sup>r</sup> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-181" data-page="181" data-folio="89" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n180_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 89">[p. 89]</button> aboute this busines giue vnto the said Comitties a Catlogue or scrowle of all the names of such as they shall soe sett on worke. And to conclude yt is furder agreed by &amp; betweene the said p̄ties that yf att any time in the doing of this service the said Porters, or any of or for them shall be founde negligent or vnfaithfull, that yt shall be free for the said Comitties to make choyse of others att their wills &amp; pleasure. Vnto the p̄formance of the same wee &amp; euʳy of vs doe binde oʳ selues by theis p̄sentꬴ. wherevnto we haue sett oʳ handꬴ the 10th of May 1606.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">THE AGREEMENT w<sup>th</sup> the Wharffenger.</h3><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">The agreem<sup>t</sup> w<sup>th</sup> the wharfenger.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">His Byll</span> Wittnesseth. That yt is couenanted concluded &amp; agreed betweene Thomas Watson wharfenger of Custome howse &amp; woll keys, &amp; the Comitties for the East India Companie in forme followinge. viz. That the said Comitties, doe promyse to pay vnto the said Tho. Watson or his assignes for lighteridge wharffedg and Cranadge all &amp; other thingꬴ necessarie to this effect the some of 3<sup>d</sup> for euʳy bagg baile Canister hoggeshead or Caske. In Considerac̃on whereof the said <strong>T W.</strong> doth for himselfe &amp; his Assignes Couenaunte p̄myse &amp; agree to and w<sup>th</sup> the said Comitties That he the said <strong>T: W:</strong> att his owne p̄p Coste &amp; chardges shall finde sufficient stanch &amp; stronge lighters and able Craines and men for carryinge &amp; Crayinge of the said Companies goodes now to be vnladen out of their shipps latelie arriued in the Riuer of Thames, And furder to satisfie the said Companie for whatsoeuʳ damadge shall happen vnto the same goodes by the insufficiencie of any th lighters or Craines, Crayne Ropes or slynge he shall sett a worke or by the default or vnfaithfullnes of any p̄son whatsoeuʳ he shall ymploye aboute the same lighters Craines or wharffes or any of them. And further to carrie abourd the said Companies shipps in his said lighters all such baggꬴ emptie Caske &amp; other thinge as shall be needfull to be imployed for the same goodꬴ w<sup>th</sup>out demaunding any thinge for the same. And to Conclude it is furder agreed <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-182" data-page="182" data-folio="90" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/registerofletter00eastuoft/page/n181_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 90">[p. 90]</button> by &amp; betwene the said p̄ties, that yf att any tyme in the p̄formance of the premisses the said <strong>𝔗. 𝔚.</strong> shall be found to deale otherwyse then shall be fittinge for the good of the Companie: Or yf any other will serue the Companie in this respect from blackwall better cheepe then is formerlie agreed vpon That then and in such case it shalbe free for the said Comitties to make choyse of others, or to ymploie the said <strong>𝔗. 𝔚.</strong> still att their will and pleasures vnto the p̄formance whereof we doe binde o<sup>r</sup> selues by this o<sup>r</sup> present writtinge. And in Witnes of the same, we haue therevnto sett o<sup>r</sup> hande the 10<sup>th</sup> of May 1606.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">A seuerall lre wth the Bills &amp; inuoyces to each Shipp from Grauesend.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Oueinge</span> freind mr Kealing<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-04-fn-155" href="#sec-04-fn-155" data-fn-target="sec-04-fn-155" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> heereinclosed we doe send you the Bill of lading of the money and appell laden in that shipp the Suzan together w<sup>th</sup> a generall Invoice aswell of all the money &amp; goode in the 4 shipps as alsoe of all that w<sup>ch</sup> form<sup>r</sup>lie remaned in the Countrie of the East Indies to thend both you and all the ffactors there may take knowledge &amp; see what they haue in chardge for the better ordering and disposeing whereof both you &amp; they are to follow such direc̃cõn as we haue alredie sufficientlie &amp; att lardge geuen you in o<sup>r</sup> lres &amp; Comission wherein we nothinge doubt of yo<sup>r</sup> due care &amp; Considerac̃on eu<sup>r</sup>y one of you in his place in discharg of yo<sup>r</sup> duties accordinge to that trust we doe repose in you. Thus wishing you a faire winde to be bound we comend vs vnto you, &amp; Comitt you &amp; all yo<sup>r</sup> Companie to the most safe p̄tec̃cõn of the highest whoe send you a safe &amp; speedie passadge to yo<sup>r</sup> desired porte &amp; graunte vs a happie meetinge to gode glorie &amp; to o<sup>r</sup> Comforte &amp;c. In Grauesend the 25<sup>th</sup> of March 1604. Signed by S<sup>r</sup> Thomas Smyth.</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-04-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">At a Court held on the 9th Jan. 1600/1 it was ordered “that a present information” and complaint shalbe made to the Ll of the Councell of suche as have disapointed “the preparation of the voyage that they may be called before ther Lordshippes to” receave suche order as to ther honors shalbe thought meet.” As the result of this application, the above order was issued, which was “read to the genralty” at the next meeting of the Company and entered at length on the minutes.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The name of James Lancaster is familiar as that of the successful leader of the Company’s first venture to the Indies. Of his early life little is known. He is said to have been a native of Basingstoke, and to have been at one time in Portugal as a soldier, and afterwards as a merchant. In 1591 he took part in Captain Raymond’s ill-fated expedition to the East Indies, of which he was one of the few survivors. He was next engaged in an enterprize to the coast of Brazil, where an attack was made on the Portuguese settlement of Pernambuco. He took an active part in the formation of the East India Company, and was one of the original directors, or ‘‘committees’’ as they were then called. Of the voyage now undertaken, as also of his two previous voyages, a full account is given in Mr. C. R. Markham’s <em>Voyages of Sir James Lancaster</em> (Hakluyt Society, 1877). On his return, Lancaster was knighted by King James; and thenceforward he appears as an active and trusted member of the Company, whose advice was constantly sought and listened to with the utmost deference. He died in June, 1618.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">1601.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This Commission “conceaved by the Comitties” was read at a General Court, on the 10th Feb. 160 <sup>0</sup>/<sub>1</sub>, and was “well lykd of and agreed to be written in fowre” severall partes and vnto everie part the Comon seale of the Companie to be added “to thend that in everie ship one of the said Comissions may be kepte in the handes” of the principall m<sup>r</sup>chauntes of everie ship.” (<em>Court Minutes.</em>)</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Brother to Sir Henry and David Middleton. He commanded the <em>Hector</em> in this voyage, and died at Bantam in the spring of 1603.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Commander of the <em>Assention</em>. Accidentally killed on the outward voyage at the Bay of Antongil in Madagascar by the guns of the <em>Dragon</em> whilst a salute was being fired.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is evidently the letter from the Lords of the Council, bearing date the 26th October, 1600, which was read at a General Courte on the 30th of that month, and in which “besides the warraunt of incoragem<sup>t</sup> geaven to the Companie to “proceade It was notid unto them that the action vndertaken by them was a publike “action and not to be dallied w<sup>th</sup>all and therfore did require that the names of such “as did ether w<sup>th</sup>drawe themselfes or practise to w<sup>th</sup>drawe others from proceading “thein shuld be certefyed to ther Lorshipes that they might examine the reason of “ther discoragem<sup>t</sup>.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Paul Bannyng, Alderman, was prominent among the promoters of the Company, and was one of the original committees named in the Charter. One of the ships employed in the <em>First Voyage</em>—the <em>Susan</em>—was purchased from him; and from 1600 to 1602 he acted with Alderman Holliday as treasurer to the Company.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sir Leonard Holliday or Haliday, Alderman, was Sheriff in 1595-96, and Lord Mayor in 1605-06. From 1600 to 1602 he was one of the treasurers of the Company, and he was nominated in the Charter as one of the first committees. In 1603 he was knighted by King James I. Six years later, he was put in nomination for the post of governor of the Company, but failed to secure election. He died 9th Jan. 1611/12.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sir Thomas Smyth, the first Governor of the Company, was the second son of Thomas Smith or Smyth, of Westenhanger, in Kent, for many years one of the Farmers of the Customs. Thomas succeeded his father in this post, and became one of the leading citizens of the metropolis. He was an active member, and for some time Governor, of the Muscovy Company, and in 1604 was sent on an embassy to that country. He was also at various periods Treasurer for the Virginian Plantations, and Governor of the French, Levant, and Sommer-Island Companies, and of the Company of Merchants Discoverers of the North-West Passage. He is, however, best remembered for his long and prominent connection with the East India Company, of which he may be described as virtually the founder. He was named Governor in the Charter (1600), but early in the new year his fortunes underwent a temporary eclipse, and he had to resign the chair to Alderman Watts. He had been accused of participation in Essex’s rash rebellion, and for a time he and his wife were held prisoners in the Tower. He succeeded in clearing himself from the charge, but it was not till the Queen’s death that the Company ventured to place him again in the position of Governor. Henceforward he reigned supreme, being re-elected year after year, sometimes in spite of his protests. At last, in 1621, he insisted on giving up his post, much to the regret of the Company, and of all interested in maritime discovery or enterprise. He died 4th September, 1625.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the providing of bread or any other necessaries “there is appointed to “goe to Dartmowthe &amp; Plymowthe, Thomas Baker and Robarte Pope to Ioyne w<sup>th</sup> “capteine Iohn Davies ffor providing all things there.” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 4th Oct. 1600.) A commission to this effect is entered on the minutes of the 16th idem. Later on (11th Nov. 1600) we find committees appointed “to make suyte to the Lord treasurer “for a lycence to transport provisions in the west countrey according to a letter “of M<sup>r</sup> In<sup>o</sup> Sandy,” and the present document is, no doubt, the result of this application.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Unsigned but evidently from Lord Buckhurst.—<em>See</em> p. 28.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This “order from the LL of her Ma<sup>es</sup> most honorable privie Councell” of the 10th April, 1601, was read at a General Court held on the following day. It resulted, as stated on page 18, in the names of three persons being “presented to the LL;” one of these made his peace with the Company, but the remaining two were disfranchised and others admitted in their places.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The following is the account of this coinage given in Ruding’s <em>Annals of the Coinage of Great Britain</em> (London, 1817):— ‘By virtue of a Commission dated January 11, in the same year (43 Eliz.), ‘Money was made of a kind unknown to the British Mint either before or since ‘her time, for it was by law exportable, and intended for the use of the East ‘India Company. It bore on one side the Queen’s arms, and on the other a ‘portcullis; and was called either Indian Money, from the purpose for which it ‘was struck, or Portcullis Money, from the device impressed upon it. The ‘weight of it was regulated according to the respective weights of the Spanish <span class="folio-turn">[p. 14]</span> “Piastre, or piece of eight Reas, and the half, the quarter, and half-quarter of “the same, though they are now usually called the Crown, Half-Crown, Shilling and “Sixpence. Some Coinage of this sort was necessary; for the Queen, when she first “incorporated the East India Company, would not permit them to transport the King “of Spain’s Silver Coins to the East Indies, though she was frequently solicited by “the merchants. The reason which they assigned to induce her to grant this per- “mission, determined her to strike Coins for the particular purpose of circulation “in Asia. They represented to her Majesty, that her Silver Coin and Stamp were “not known in the East Indies, which they supposed would induce her to grant them “a license to send thither what Silver they pleased. The Queen and her Privy “Council replied, that, for the very reason alledged, it was her fixed and unalter- “able resolution not to permit them to send the Coin of the King of Spain, or of “any foreign Prince, to India; and that no Silver should be exported by her “merchants, but only such as should be coined with her effigies and picture on the “one side, and the portcullis on the other, of the just weight and fineness of the “Spanish pieces of eight, and pieces of four Rials. Her prudent reason for this “was, that her name and effigies might be hereafter respected by the Asiatics, and “she be known as great a Prince as the King of Spain.”<br class="fn-para">Ruding notes that “notwithstanding this determination, the pieces have only “her arms on the Obverse;” and he further quotes a passage from Malynes’ <em>Lex Mercatoria</em> to the effect that “of these pieces were coined in the Tower of London for “a trial (in January 1600 [? 1601]) some six thousand Pounds, which could not be “made current in the East Indies because the Spanish pieces of eight Royals had “been before that time counterfeited by other nations, which made the East Indians “to doubt of our Coin, although without cause.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“It is ordered at this Assemblie that a Warraunt shalbe sent to the persons “heervnder named that have hetherto denyed to bringe in ther Contributions, “subscribed under the hand of the Governor and 4 of the Comitties w<sup>ch</sup> warraunt “shalbe shewed vnto them by a Purscvant to thend that vppon ther refusall to pay “they may be carried before the LL: or els vppon ther assent to pay they may be “appointed to come to M<sup>r</sup> Governor to geave ther Wordes to pay the same to the “Threr the tenor of w<sup>ch</sup> warraunt doeth followe in these words videlt: . . . .” (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 22nd April, 1601). This measure seems to have had the desired effect, for only in three cases was it found necessary to “convent” the defaulters before the Council. One of these, Hugh Hamersley, made his peace with the Company and paid in his subscription; the other two, John Bate and Robert Bowyer, were disfranchised.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See</em> page 12.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">These are the names of the persons to whom the warrant was addressed.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Afterwards became a prominent member of the Company, serving as a “committee” for a considerable number of years; and was nominated, but unsuccessfully, for the post of governor on the death of Sir William Haliday in 1624. He was Sheriff in 1619, and Lord Mayor in 1628; and was for some time governor of the Levant Company.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Consul at Aleppo <em>circa</em> 1611-14. In a letter to the Company in August, 1613, he expresses his desire to do them service, “being a freeman of their charter though no adventurer” (<em>O. C.</em> 109).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">On the 4th Dec. 1600, it was ordered that “m<sup>r</sup> Staper Capten Lancaster m<sup>r</sup> Tho.” Allabaster and Richard Wright shall conferre together touching suche lres as shalbe “solicited from her ma<sup>ie</sup> to suche princes and Potentates as are in the places of the” Este wher trade shalbe sought;” and on the 12th Feb. 1601, a warrant was passed for the payment to “M<sup>r</sup> W<sup>m</sup> Segar one of the herraults for the wryting of her ma<sup>es</sup> Lres” to the kinges of the East Indies the some of xiij<sup>li</sup> vj<sup>s</sup> viij<sup>d</sup> and xij<sup>d</sup> for a boxe bought “by him to putt in the same lres.” From another entry we learn that there were six of these letters taken out in the first voyage. They seem to have been in identical terms, with a blank left for the factors to fill in the name of the particular king to whom the letter was to be delivered. It will be seen that the text now given differs slightly from that printed in Bruce’s <em>Annals</em>, vol. I, pp. 147-150, but the variations are of no importance.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">George Waymouth was despatched by the Company in May 1602, to discover a passage to India “by the way of the Norwest.” The voyage ended in ignominious failure, the vessels returning after having got no further than the south of Greenland. See <em>The Dawn of British Trade to the East Indies</em>, Preface, pp. xvi.-xxii.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">It will be noted that China and “Cataya” are spoken of as separate countries. See <em>Report on the Old Records of the India Office</em> (W. H. Allen &amp; Co., 1891), page 168.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Fraud or deceit,—strictly an act of conspiracy between two or more persons to defraud others.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not entered; but its contents may be gathered from the Company’s answer on the next page.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr., afterwards Sir, Julius Cæsar, the son of a doctor of Italian extraction, who had settled in London, and had risen to notice as a Court physician, was appointed in 1583 judge of the Admiralty Court. Five years later he was admitted master of the chancery in ordinary, and in 1591 Elizabeth conferred on him the post of master <span class="folio-turn">[p. 26]</span> of requests. He was knighted by James I. on the 20th May, 1603. In 1606 he was made chancellor and under-treasurer of the exchequer, and in the following year was sworn of the Privy Council. Seven years later, he succeeded to the post of master of the rolls, for which he had long been waiting; and the rest of his life is one unbroken record of active state employment. He died 18th April, 1636, and was buried in the church of Great St. Helen’s, Bishopsgate, where his monument may yet be seen. His legal knowledge appears to have been hardly adequate to the important posts he was called upon to fill, and this point did not escape the notice of his enemies; but even they were forced to admit the excellence of his private character. His charity was unbounded, and during his early career his generosity to impecunious suitors in his court was carried to an extent that threatened seriously to embarrass his own resources. He was Cecil’s right-hand man at a time of great difficulty in regard to the national finances, and his whole career seems to have been that of a useful public servant.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Priaman, a town on the west coast of Sumatra, north of Padang.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Markham’s <em>Voyages of Sir James Lancaster</em> (Hakluyt Society, 1877), p. 91.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">They.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The pepper referred to is evidently that brought home by the ships of the <em>First Voyage</em>. The loss of the second Court Book prevents us from following in detail the course of events.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Thomas Sackville, Lord Buckhurst, afterwards Earl of Dorset, Lord Treasurer, 1599 to 1608.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The plague was then raging in London (see page 39).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “taking”?</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A mistake for “uttered”?</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Impartial, just; as in the prayer in the English Church Liturgy, “that they “may truly and <em>indifferently</em> minister justice.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is no signature, but the reply shows that Lord Buckhurst was the writer, and also supplies the date,</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. Thomas Bramley was at this time Deputy Governor of the Company, having been chosen to succeed Mr. William Romney (see page 46) in that post on the 26th January, 1603.<br class="fn-para">The following extract from the <em>Calendar of State Papers, E. Indies, 1513-1616</em>, p. 139, continues the story:—<br class="fn-para">“1603, 18 Nov. London. Thos. Alabaster to Sec. Cecil. M<sup>r</sup> Bramley and M<sup>r</sup> Cham- “bers sent by the East India Company to Court, through a letter from the Lord “Treasurer, about liberty for sale of their pepper, which it seemed he would stay, “unless they would buy the King’s, or it were otherwise sold; the Company will give “in their reasons. Intreats his favour towards them; the extreme scarcity of money “prevents them buying it.” (<em>Extract from Domestic Corresp., Jac. I., Vol. IV., no. 86, Cal., p. 54</em>.)<br class="fn-para">The correspondence is continued on page 42.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Before leaving Bantam on the return voyage, Lancaster arranged that the pinnace should make a trading voyage to the Moluccas and endeavour to settle a factory there. Accordingly on the 6th March, 1602/3, the pinnace set sail, but after battling for nearly two months against contrary winds, was forced to return with its object unaccomplished.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Became in 1605 chief at Bantam, and held that post till December, 1608, when he went home as captain of the <em>Dragon</em>. In 1611 he commanded the <em>Hector</em> in the <em>Eighth Voyage</em> under Saris, returning to England two years later. The following year he married the widow of Captain Hawkins, and shortly after was admitted a freeman of the Company gratis, in recognition of his long service. In 1615 he applied to be entertained for another voyage, but was refused. Two years later we find both him and his wife at Surat, having apparently been permitted to take passage in one of the ships of Captain Pring’s fleet. He returned to England with Sir Thomas Roe in 1619, leaving his wife in India. In 1620 he was entertained as a principal factor for the Moluccas, and in the course of the voyage was placed in charge of the <em>Lesser James</em> until her arrival at Bantam. He was then sent to Amboyna to succeed George Muschamp as agent, and there, with nine of his fellow countrymen, he suffered torture and death at the hands of the Dutch in what is known as the Massacre of Amboyna, 27th Feb., 1622/3.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Factor in the <em>First Voyage</em>; died at Bantam, 14th April, 1604.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Factor in the <em>First Voyage</em>; died in 1603 while on the voyage to Banda in the pinnace.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Died 17th July, 1603.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Morgan died 26th April, 1603, and Starkie on the 30th June following. Edmond Scott lived to come home with Sir Henry Middleton in the <em>Second Voyage</em>, and to write an interesting account of the early years of the English settlement at Bantam, which is printed in <em>Purchas</em>. In July, 1608, he was elected a committee, but was removed on the 28th March, 1609, “for his public abuse of William Harrison on the Exchange.” Then followed a wrangle with the Company as to moneys due to him, which was referred to arbitration but apparently without satisfactorily disposing of the matter. In 1619 he was made an auditor of the Company, and this is the last notice that can be traced of him.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Lancaster’s copy of these instructions (from which most probably the present copy was made), would have been signed in this way by the persons addressed, as an acknowledgment that they had made themselves acquainted with its contents.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Tumult or uproar. “Look here, and at thy sovereign leisure, read The garboils she awaked.”—(<em>Antony &amp; Cleopatra</em>, Act I, Sc. 3.)</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">These letters were taken out by a Dutch fleet, which reached Bantam in December, 1604.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">These dates differ from those given in the preceding letter.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the previous correspondence see pp. 27-32.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">William, afterwards Sir William, Romeny, or Romney, haberdasher, was one of the original adventurers, and in September, 1599, was appointed treasurer of the proposed voyage. He was named a committee in the patent, and at the beginning of 1601 was chosen Deputy Governor of the Company. Two years later, he resigned this post on being made an Alderman. He was Sheriff in 1603-4, and in July, 1603, was knighted by King James. By the end of 1606, he had become Governor of the Company, but the actual date of his appointment is not known. His tenure of the post ended in July, 1607, when Sir Thomas Smyth became Governor. Romeny was nominated again in 1609, but was not elected. He died 25th April, 1611.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">John, afterwards Sir John, Wolstenholme, born in 1562, was a farmer of the customs, and a well-known merchant, being especially active in the promotion of voyages to the North-West. His name occurs in 1612 as a committee in the charter of incorporation of the Company of Merchants of London, discoverers of the North-West Passage; and two years later he is mentioned as having, in conjunction with Sir Thomas Smyth, paid Wright, the mathematician, to deliver courses of lectures. He was an original member of the East India Company, and in 1619 was made a committee. In July, 1628, he was nominated for Governor, but excused himself. He died 25th Nov., 1639.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sir Henry Middleton, brother of David and John Middleton, was in the <em>First Voyage</em> a factor “of the second sort.” At Acheen Lancaster made him captain of the <em>Susan</em>, and sent him to Priaman, whence he carried home a cargo of pepper. He commanded the <em>Second Voyage</em>, and was knighted on his return in 1606. Four years later, he took command of the <em>Trades Increase</em> as general of the <em>Sixth Voyage</em>, from which so much was hoped. Much time was wasted in the Red Sea, where, for a time, Middleton was a prisoner at Mocha; and even after he had escaped and had carried his ships to Surat, he returned to exact compensation for the ill-treatment <span class="folio-turn">[p. 47]</span> previously received. After an ineffectual blockade of the Turkish ports, he was compelled to abandon his purpose, and sailed for Tecco, in Sumatra. Thence he proceeded to Bantam, from which port Downton was sent home with the <em>Peppercorn.</em> The <em>Trades Increase</em>, which had excited so much admiration at the time of her launch under royal auspices, was by this time worn out and leaky, and had to be beached at Bantam, where she was afterwards set on fire by the natives and completely destroyed. Middleton, who had remained at Bantam after Downton’s departure, died there on 24th May, 1613, worn out and “hartesore” at the ill-success of his expedition.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 19.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Commanded the <em>Hector</em> in the <em>Second Voyage</em>. At Bantam he was transferred to the <em>Assention</em>, in which he made a successful voyage to Banda, and subsequently returned to England in company with the <em>Dragon</em> and <em>Hector</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Third factor on the <em>Assention</em> in the <em>First Voyage</em>. Commanded the same ship in the <em>Second Voyage</em>, until Bantam was reached, when he was removed to the <em>Hector</em>. He died shortly after, in March, 1605.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Commanded the <em>Susan</em> in the <em>Second Voyage</em> as far as Bantam, when he was removed to the <em>Hector</em> (upon the death of Styles) for the homeward voyage. Upon his return he was placed in command of the ships of the <em>Third Voyage</em>, the <em>Dragon</em> being his flag-ship. In 1615 he commanded another voyage on account of the <em>First Joint Stock</em>, upon an agreement that he was to remain chief commander in the Indies for five years (<em>O. C.</em> 369). He returned to England about two years later, and in 1618 obtained the appointment of Captain of Cowes Castle. He died towards the end of 1620.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Left at Bantam by Middleton as second in rank to Towerson. He appears to have returned to England at some date after March, 1610, and went out again in the <em>Seventh Voyage</em> with Captain Hippon. He died off Masulipatam, 8th Sept., 1611.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-056-2" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Purser of the <em>Red Dragon</em> and afterwards of the <em>Assention</em> in the <em>First Voyage</em>. <span class="folio-turn">[p. 53]</span> He went out again in the succeeding expedition, and, while the ships were at Bantam, was promoted to the command of the <em>Susan.</em> This vessel foundered on her way home, and all on board perished.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Contracts?</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Maio, one of the Cape Verd Islands.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Then?</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pœna.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Borax; in Italian, <em>borrace</em>, Low Latin, <em>boracum</em>, and Arabic, <em>buraq</em>; all these forms being derived from the Persian <em>burah</em>. Borax, or “green earth,” is the biborate of soda, imported, in its crude form, into India from Thibet under the name of tincal. Compare Balax, and Ballace, the Balas or spinel ruby, the <em>al Badakhsh</em>, and <em>Badakhshi</em> of the Arabs, imported into India from Badakhshan.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Pintadoes” were painted cotton clothes, that is chintzes [Sanskrit, <em>chitra</em>, “spotted,” “variegated,” as in <em>chitraka</em>, the “Cheeta” or “hunting-leopard,” <em>Felis jubata</em>; and <em>chital</em>, the “spotted-deer,” <em>Axis maculatus</em>] so called from the Portuguese <em>pinta</em>, “painted,” literally “spotted,” as in <em>pintado</em>, the “Guinea-fowl,” and, indeed, any “spotted” bird; thus Fryer, in his <em>Travels</em> [1698], p. 12, writes: “Gaining upon “the East with a slow pace, we met those feathered harbingers of the Cape, as “<em>Pintado-Birds, Mangosaluedos, Albetrosses</em>.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malay girdles? Or may they possibly be girdles of “jute,” the fibre of <em>Corchorus olitorius</em>, “Jew’s Mallow” [<em>maluach</em> of Job xxx. 4] and <em>C. capsularis</em>? The μολόχινα [as if ἱμάτια] of Arrian’s <em>Periplus of the Red Sea</em>, usually translated [see Vincent’s <em>Commerce and Navigation of the Ancients</em> (1807), vol. II, p. 116]: “Cotton cloths of the “colour of Mallow flowers,” were possibly these very same “malow girdles” or jute cloth-pieces. If this is a true identification, it is a most interesting one. But I have never before met with the phrase “malow-girdles;” and Arrian is the only classical writer who uses the word μολόχινα. Plautus, in the <em>Aulularia</em> (III, 10), mentions “dyers in mallow colour,”—<em>molochinarii</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Siam; or possibly Syrian, a port of Pegu, sixty miles east of “China Bukheer,” one of the delta mouths of the Irawadi.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Java girdles” are the chintzes called by the Javanese <em>batik</em>, <em>i.e.</em> “painted,” or rather “delineated”; as the cotton cloth to be decorated, after first being covered with a film of wax, poured on at the boiling point, has the design literally delineated throughout it with a sharp-pointed style, before being dipped into the dye, which colours the cloth only where the wax has been removed by the style. Then, when the dye has been fixed, the remaining wax is melted off. Sometimes this process is repeated over and over again, with a different dye each time, thus producing wonderfully elaborate designs; but the most pleasing results are always obtained from a single application of it, as, for instance, in the spriggled <em>batiks</em>, with their simple floral patterns pencilled in monochrome on the natural cream-coloured surface of the cotton cloth. These so-called “Java girdles,” including dress-pieces of all sorts, such as robes, mantles, veils, and also curtains, have been celebrated in India from the time of the first introduction of Hindu civilization into the Indian Archipelago; and in the play of <em>Málaté and Mádháva</em> [See Wilson’s <em>Theatre of the Hindus</em> (1871), vol. II. p. 74], attributed to the 8th century, <span class="sc">A.D.</span>, are expressly referred to under the phrase <em>chitra-Javanika</em>, “<em>Javanese chintz</em>”; in this place the painted curtain, or <em>peplos</em>, suspended before the adytum of the temple into which, in the third act, Makaranda retires to make up his toilet as a female.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 33.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">462.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably, as suggested by Mr. Bolton Corney, in his <em>Voyage of Sir Henry Middleton</em> (Hakluyt Society), Pernambuco-wood (called also Nicaragua-wood, Jamaica-wood, and peach-wood), an inferior kind of sappan, or brazil. Sappan is the wood of <em>Cæsalpinia Sappan</em>, a native of Southern India, its Tamil name being <em>shappu</em>, and its Malayalam name <em>shappannam</em>, both meaning “red.” From the latter was formed its Malayan name <em>sapang</em>, and its Arabic name <em>bakkam</em>; while, translated into the terminology of the mediæval commerce of Europe, <em>shappannam</em> became “brazil,” that is “blazing”-red-wood. Afterwards, a variety (<em>Cæsalpinia echinata</em>) of this brazil having been found in South America, its name was given to the vast country of the Brazils.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Haply.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The like.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently written in error for “ship.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">God’s.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Edmond Scott’s relation of events at Bantam between February, 1603, and 1605, printed in <em>Purchas</em>, Lib. III., ch. 4.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Speilberg or Spilberg, the captain of a Dutch ship, who had joined Lancaster at his departure from Acheen on an agreement that he should have a share in any prize that might be taken. He afterwards sold at Bantam the goods thus obtained, and it is to this the entry refers.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Printed in <em>Purchas</em>, Lib. V., ch. 14.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">“The weight by which they [the Achinese] sell Precious Stones is called <em>Masse</em>. <span class="folio-turn">[p. 68]</span> “10½ whereof make an ounce.”—<em>John Davis his Relation in Purchas</em>, Lib. III., ch. 1. The <em>masha</em> is the elementary weight, of from 16 to 20 grains, used immemorially by the Hindu jewellers throughout India. The names of most of the weights of the Malays and Javanese are taken from the ancient Hindu ones, as <em>rakat</em>, so named after the Hindu <em>rati</em>, and <em>tail</em> after the Hindu <em>tola</em> (<em>cf.</em> τάλαντον, <em>tolero</em>, <em>tollo</em>, <em>Tantalus</em>, <em>Atlas</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This copy differs to some extent from the version given in <em>Purchas</em>, Lib. V., ch. 14.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Purchas</em>, Lib. II., ch. 3.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Amboyna,</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy of this grant in the India Office Records (<em>O. C.</em> No. 1), but it presents no important variation. The fate of the original is not known. There is an entry in the Court Minutes for 26th February, 1614, that Sir James Lancaster, who was supposed to have it in his custody, was to be asked for it, to send to the Indies (<em>Cal. E. I. State Papers</em>, 1513-1616, p. 279); but there is no further mention of the subject.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">“Nowe” in <em>O. C.</em> 1.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy of this note in the <em>O. C.</em> Series (N<sup>o</sup>. 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Lantore, Lontor or Groot Banda.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">In a map of Banda given in a <em>Histoire de la Navigation aux Indes Orientales</em> (1609) the towns of <em>Ortattan, Combeer</em> and <em>Labetack</em> are shewn, the two former being on the island of Lantore, and the third on the island of Neira.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pulo Rosagijn.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">7.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pulo Ai?</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">8.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pulo Ron?</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">9.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently some variant of the Dutch <em>kleed</em>, a cloth, dress, or habit.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">10.</a> <span class="fn-body">This happens to be an interesting blunder, although due merely to the transcriber’s preoccupation with “the blessed word Mesopotamia,” for from the earliest times there was a close connection between the countries on the Persian Gulf, and the Coromandel Coast; and both Nineveh and Masulipatam mean “Fish-town.” Mosul, which gives its name to muslin, is the modern representative of ancient Nineveh, but the word has no connection with <em>masuli</em> in Masuli-patam, and would appear to be the Arabic word <em>mahsul</em>, “revenue,” here meaning “Revenue”-station.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">11.</a> <span class="fn-body">Madinrens (<em>O. C.</em> 35). It is impossible to identify all of these local denominations of piece goods; but for some of them see the notes on the two following pages.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See page 96, note 2.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy of this and the following notes in the document already referred to (<em>O. C.</em> 35). It is there described as “a Noate written in Duch A<sup>o</sup> 1606 &amp; Translated by one of the Companyes factors at Bantam.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Calico.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">So called from the Persian <em>bafta</em>, “woven”: it was originally a kind of calico, chiefly obtained from Baroch.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">A silk cloth, so called from the Canarese and Malayalam <em>pattuda</em>, “silk cloth.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps <em>sari</em>, the cloth used as a robe by Hindu women.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malaysia (<em>O. C.</em> 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This word means “breadth,” and signifies a cloth of one breadth. The <em>do-patta</em>, or sheet of two breadths, is the only garment of the women of the lower orders in Bengal.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Theis (<em>O. C.</em> 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">That is <em>muza’fari</em>, “saffron-y,” originally a “saffron”-dyed stuff. Yellow has immemorially been the colour of joy among the Hindus, and in Hindu art Vishnu and Rama are always apparelled in bright yellow; dull yellow (<em>bag-wa</em>, “god”-colour) being the colour of the vestments of the Saiva ascetics. Red also is held by the Hindus to be a joyous colour, and is always worn at marriages; and both colours are regarded by them as sacred to the sun, and to the East, or place of sun rise, and the South, or “right hand path” of the sun; red being the colour usually appropriated to the former and yellow to the latter, although some sects reverse this allocation. Yellow was the sacred colour also of the Babylonian and Assyrian Anunit or Ishtar, and to this day it continues to be the favourite colour of Jewish women. Significantly enough the early Christians, in the same way that they made Friday— the day of Ishtar and Venus and Friya—the unlucky day of the week, made yellow the colour of sin and crime, painting the doors of felons yellow, and compelling the Jews to wear yellow, just as the Mahomedans compel them, and the Christians of Egypt and Syria, &amp;c., to wear dark blue, which is the uncanny colour and the colour of mourning throughout Anterior and Southern Asia. As of Anunit or Ishtar, yellow was the sacred colour of Aphrodite-Venus and Artemis-Diana, both derivatives from Ishtar; and from the earliest ages it was the colour most affected by the women of Greece. In Homer Eōs is not only “rosy-fingered” (ῥοδοδάκτυλος, <em>Odyssey</em> II., 1), but “saffron-mantled” (κροκόπεπλος, <em>Iliad</em> VIII., 1, and XIX., 1); as are Enyō—one of the three Graiæ—in Hesiod (<em>Theognis</em>, 273), and the Muses in Alcman (74); while the references and allusions in the Greek and Latin poets to the saffron-coloured robes so fashionable among the ladies of ancient Athens and Rome are innumerable, as in the <em>Agamemnon</em> of Æschylus, the <em>Lysistrata</em>, <em>Thesmophoriazusæ</em>, and <em>Ecclesiazusæ</em> of Aristophanes, and the plays of Plautus, who in his <em>Aulularia</em> (III., 10) mentions not only “dyers in mallow colour” (see p. 59, note 2), but “dyers in saffron”—incedunt infectores crocotarii—their stealthy, enticing approach, like that of a Bombay <em>borah</em>, or “<em>box-wallah</em>,” indicating the reckless passion of the Roman women for this colour, in the belief that it gave an irresistible power to the natural charm of their sex over men: in which they were justified, to judge from the sweet, winsome loveliness of the young high-caste Hindu women of Western India, as they appear abroad on gala occasions issuing forth in their innumerable twinkling slippers of Telmont yellow, rosy red, or Mazarine blue, and enrobed in lustrous saffrontinctured <em>saris</em> of soft fluttering silk, rendered still more radiant and ethereal by the sparkle and sheen of diamond and pearl.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">China (<em>O. C.</em> 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Snaphances (Dutch <em>snaphaan</em>), <em>i.e.</em> guns with spring locks, as opposed to the old match locks.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pipe Canns (<em>O. C.</em> 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>O. C.</em> 35 adds “some playne haftes.”</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Tanga</em> (Sanskrit <em>tanka</em>, “a weight”—of silver), the chief silver coin of the Mogol Emperors of Delhi, representing the <em>dirhem</em> (drachma) of the Arabs, and the “rupee” (Sanskrit <em>rupya</em>, “beautified”—silver) of modern India. The term is still applied to a silver coin in Turkestan, worth about 7½<em>d.</em> The <em>padma-tanka</em> of Southern India was a gold coin, and in Tamil the word <em>tanka</em> means, specifically, “weighty,”—as gold. Universally in India a mint is designated <em>tankasala</em>, commonly shortened to <em>tangsal</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Lowlo, Lossidi (<em>O. C.</em> 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Tzidle (<em>O. C.</em> 35). See note 11 on page 73.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Also spelled armozeen, armosyn, and armesie (from the Italian <em>ormisino</em>, “sarcanet,” “taffeta-sarcenet,” <em>see</em> Florio’s <em>Worlde of Wordes</em>); originally a stout black silk much used in the sixteenth to eighteenth centuries for making “Geneva gowns” and for funeral scarfs, but afterwards dyed of various colours.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Botton (<em>O. C.</em> 35). <em>Sindi</em> cloth (σινδών of the Greeks, and <em>sadin</em> of Proverbs xxxi. 24, Isaiah iii. 23, and Judges xiv. 12 and 13.)</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Quarl</em> or <em>quarrel</em>, a square (Low Lat. <em>quadrillus, quarellus</em>, N. C.</em> 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A Chinese weight, the denomination of which, <em>kati</em>, is Malay, equal to a little over one pound avoirdupois.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">537<sup>li</sup>¾ (<em>O. C.</em> 35).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-132" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-132" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Coem (<em>O. Socotrina</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-143" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-143" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">A saker was a piece of ordnance of 3½ inches bore, with a range of about 1700 paces.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-144" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" king of Calicut and the dependent countryside.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-146" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-146" aria-label="Return to <em>Nares’ Glossary</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-151" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-151" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pistols.</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-152" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-152" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hamburg (Hamborough).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-153" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-153" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Knight’s voyage in search of the North-West Passage was undertaken in 1606. <em>The Hopewell</em> crossed the Atlantic in safety, and reached the coast of Labrador, where Knight and three others landed, and were never seen or heard of again. The survivors returned to England in September, 1606. Knight’s journal of the expedition, which is still preserved among the India Office Records, has been published by Mr. C. R. Markham as an appendix to his <em>Voyages of Sir James Lancaster</em> (Hakluyt Society, 1877).</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-154" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-154" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The home-coming ships of Middleton’s expedition (<em>Second Voyage</em>, 1604-6). The <em>Red Dragon</em> was off Plymouth on 2nd May, 1606, and both she and the <em>Assention</em> were in the Downs on the sixth of the same month. (<em>Voyage of Sir Henry Middleton</em>, Hakluyt Society, 1857.)</span></li><li id="sec-04-fn-155" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-04-fn-155" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Then setting out for the Indies in command of the <em>Susan</em> in the <em>Second Voyage</em> (see note 2, page 51).</span></li></ol></section>
